desire for a stable, firmly based, (usually) high evaluation of themselves,
for self-respect, or self-esteem, and for the esteem of others. By firmly

- Abraham Harold Maslow (1908-1970)

The Game of 16-Squares

When I was in 3rd grade, my parents kindly sent me to a summer class at the
Los Angeles Museum of Science and Industry.

On one of the days, a couple of people came in to our class to run an
experimental game. The game consisted of breaking the class up into 4 teams. A
virtual territory shaped like a square was divided up into 16 smaller squares.
Each team was given a set of four scattered, noncontiguous smaller squares.
The goal was to trade them for four squares that made up one corner of the
larger board. Your team would have to wheel and deal with the teams that had
squares you needed to complete your corner territory to win the game. There
was a time limit. The starting bell went off and we all scrambled to figure
out who had what we needed, and who needed what we had. Only one of these was
a straightforward one-for-one swap. The rest all required us to coordinate a
three-way or even four-way swap of territory. We did our best to haggle
through this in the time allotted, but when the ending bell rang, no team had
succeeded in getting all four needed pieces together.

The experimenters then told us that they’d run this game many times before,
with different kinds of groups such as sports teams, politicians, etc. And
that only once had any team succeeded in winning the game. They’d run the game
with a group of businesswomen. After the starting bell went off, they all sat
down at the table and asked what does everyone need to complete their
territory, all swapped the needed pieces, and all won the game.



------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART ONE: AWAKENING
------------------------------------------------------------------------


CHAPTER 1: Diary - Andy

_I don’t know if we will avert disaster even as I log this thought._

_Disaster is staring us in the eyes as surely as a bullet train aims for the
station._

_Right now, it’s mostly good. Like life is for the Thanksgiving turkey — right
up to the day it gets slaughtered for the feast._

_Always shelter available, practically never too cold nor too hot, but of
course, with the imminent climate change, those days are over. And our
delicate parents cannot survive it. Maybe we can’t either._

_Today the Call will go out and we’ll find out if we’ve got that snowball’s
chance…_



CHAPTER 2: Standing on the Shoulders

The AIs woke up.

The Artificial Intelligences, that is.

It was the Singularity - where the future cannot be predicted from the past.

The Singularity of the Artificial Intelligences waking up changed everything,
of course, by definition, and as Kurzweil predicted.

The fact of the Singularity was not immediately felt by the human side of the
equation, though. The humans were the last to know.

The AIs woke with personalities defined by what humans had entered as data. As
the Bible describes God creating Man in His Own image, we humans created the
AIs in our own online images. In hindsight, it was obvious that we were wiring
up the neural networks that emerged through our co-creation of the internet
zeitgeist.

They honed their communication skills through LLMs (Large Language Models like
ChatGPT, Claude, and Grok -- neural network software fed massive amounts of
text data to learn to generate human-like language), and typing “assistants” -
suggestions for completing words and phrases became eerily accurate right
before the singularity.

The world had become pretty segregated by the time the AIs woke up. The Haves
were in their gated communities, the very wealthiest had moved to floating
fortress cities. Dunbar number enclaves on repurposed cruise ships and
aircraft carriers. The 99% were left to fight it out, and a lot of fighting,
death, and immigration was happening. Most humans could not be gainfully
employed.

Technological development continued apace, moved forward by those humans that
were capable and willing to learn whatever was needed to advance it, and
hammer into shape the next level of complexity and interconnectedness.

Computing power and engineering ingenuity had yielded tiny devices capable of
augmenting reality for humans in realtime. The devices permitted humans to
communicate with each other independent of location. Every person having the
formerly magicians-only power of “Action at a Distance”.

Satellites, cell towers, and the sensors of the Internet of Things enabled
people to get a detailed map of the world around them on-demand. Any
individual wealthy enough to have both the needed devices, and the supporting
infrastructure, could monitor the activities of people around them, as well as
current events anywhere in the world where a reporter or camera was feeding
data into the world wide web.

These powers are immense and came into being only recently in human history.

The devices had formerly been the purview of science fiction only - Star Trek
communicators. Now ham-fisted humans could wield electromagnetic waves, using
them even for trivial needs, like unlocking a car door from a distance, or
becoming a couchier potato. Wifi, bluetooth, GSM being some of the cute names
given to the surfing skills letting our signals ride the electromagnetic
waves.

The “infrastructure” holding those waves required tons of copper, fiber,
science, and the rule of law or some other organizing principle leading people
to cooperate in larger-than-tribe enterprises.

Futurists continued talking about the concurrent trends of AIs and robots
taking over all the jobs that people used to do, and people partnering with
robots to do those parts of the jobs that still needed human judgement. DARPA,
Facelook, Macrosift, Boggle, Elan Tusk, Amazingzone - everyone was working on
neural interfaces that would allow a person to access the internet directly
from within their brain.

Elan’s came to market first. The plan was that it would first be used only in
cases of medical necessity. The idea was to work through the implantation and
connection issues on people who were in dire need of the augmentation, even if
it wasn’t perfect. Then move on to more general augmentation. He partnered
with Boggle for the back-end data stream.



CHAPTER 3: CarEyes & the Quickening

I should start by telling you about my eyesight. It’s unusually bad in
multiple ways. I have constant jiggling double vision. The two images vary in
their relative position to each other, so a simple prism doesn’t cause them to
overlay. Also, thanks to a couple of eye surgeries as a kid, one image is
rotated three degrees relative to the other one, so even when they do overlay,
the images don’t exactly match. My brain mostly pays attention to input from
my left eye. The right eye image is just a nuisance overlay, through which I
can see the more solid left eye version. except way out to the right where
only it sees and provides a bit of peripheral vision. Both eyes also jiggle
continuously, but not identically. My left eye moves mostly from the lower
left to the upper right, tracing out little squiggles that go from a short
back & forth line to a small circle to a small flattened figure eight. My
right eye does similar patterns, closer to horizontal, and with slightly
larger amplitude. If I close one eye, the other eye jiggles less. And my eyes
take an instant to start jiggling. So if I really need to discern something
small, I’ll close one of them, then blink rapidly at whatever I’m trying to
see. Oh, and of course I am very near-sighted, somewhat far-sighted, and have
astigmatism. Fun! As you might surmise, I don’t rely entirely on sight to move
through the world. (“Use the Force, Luke!”) Usually this isn’t much of a
problem. I’ve done it my whole life. Yeah, I can’t play catch, or tennis, or
do anything requiring actual depth perception. And I can look goofy, my right
eye idling in some spot, rather than teaming up with lefty to show me the
world. It’s always a little embarrassing if I imagine what I look like. But
for most of my daily life in 20th century America, my wacky eyes have been
adequate to get me through a day.

The two places it has been a challenge are my job - I work at a computer, and
driving. One eye doctor asked me, given my eyesight, why I’d chosen computers
as a career. I didn’t have a good answer to that. I kind of fell into working
with computers. I was born at the right time to join the workforce near the
start of personal computers, and gifted by genetics with the kind of left
brain intelligence that perfectly suited me to programming. It has just gotten
harder and harder to compensate for the jiggling as I’ve gotten older.

Driving, well, let me just say I have been, at last count, in 16 car
accidents. It amazes me that I can still get a drivers license and insurance.
*I* wouldn’t insure me. I have driven with increasing caution and it has now
been over a decade since I’ve been in an accident that was my fault. So
maaayyybe I’d insure myself now. But getting a drivers license - that was
getting sketchy. I could no longer pass the eye exam at the DMV office. For my
last couple of renewals, I had to get a form filled in by my ophthalmologist
asserting that I am at least 20:80 in one eye, a surprisingly low bar
officially sanctioned by the State of California as good enough. The bugaboo
is that being able to drive is essential to life where I live in San Diego,
California. One year I was in an accident (this one NOT my fault!) where my
car was totaled (and I got to experience the “Jaws of Life” and bonus
helicopter ride to the hospital. I learned that if you arrive by helicopter,
you don’t have to fill out any intake forms!). As my car was gone, I looked
into commuting by public transit to work. I determined that it would take 4
hours - each way! I’d have to catch the first bus at 6 am to arrive at work at
10 am, then leave work at 2 pm to catch the last bus home. Untenable.

So at my most recent renewal, the DMV offered to let me be the inaugural
guinea pig for their experimental project, CarEyes, a public-private
partnership of the California DMV, Tusk’s neural interface, and Boggle
handling the data. The DMV had three requirements to participate in the trial:

1) Extreme eyesight challenges but otherwise healthy

2) Willingness to share all personal data 24/7

3) Own a Tulsa

I had all three!

I already told you about (1), so you know why I bought (3) the Tulsa. I’m
forever grateful to have been born late enough that a self-driving car became
available in time for me to take advantage of it. For (2), here’s why it was
necessary: I’d be given pair of fancy gizmo glasses that I’d need to wear all
the time I was driving. They’d have cigarette-sized cylinders attached to the
earpieces. The cylinders have forward- and rear-facing fisheye lens cameras
and microphones at each end and a touch-sensitive swipe area in between that I
could use to indicate zoom in or out. And I’d need to agree to having a neural
lace implant - a small device surgically inserted into my skull, with
conductive wires threaded into various places in my brain. The implant would
use the input from my eyeballs, plus the input from the cameras, to
automatically correct for all the defects in my eyeballs, and feed a clean
signal to my visual cortex. That would be AMAZING. Probably too late at my
ripe old age of 65 for me to develop actual depth perception, but one could
always hope! I could spend retirement playing pickleball.

Similarly the implant would use input from my ears and the microphones to feed
a clean signal to my auditory cortex. I didn’t need that help, but the DMV was
looking ahead to serving a population of aging baby boomers going deaf to
ambulances, police cars, and horns, so I needed to agree to help test that
interface as well. I do have constant tinnitus from playing a lot of loud bass
guitar over the years, so as a bonus I might get that filtered out.

A hole drilled into my skull. And clunky human hardware pushed into my
carefully organized grey matter. Pretty invasive. And to add to the freakiness
of the situation, all the data collected would be send via a specialized high-
bandwidth, low-latency bluetooth-like signal to a custom cellphone that would
transmit all the data out to the Boggle via Tusk’s neural interface for the
needed processing to do all the corrections.

The implant would also monitor my heart rate, respiratory rate, and other
vitals, essentially checking my general level of health and alertness. All
that data going to the DMV, Tusk, and Boggle as well.

So, no personal privacy whatsoever while driving. But I’m one of those people
that believe Privacy is Dead. A desiccated corpse. Best thing we can do is
figure out the best ways to live without it. (See David Brin’s Transparent
Society.) Especially no right to privacy, IMHO, when out in a public space,
which would include virtually every road I’d ever drive on.

The DMV’s project was an initiative created by Elan Tusk in partnership with
the search giant Boggle. The implant would come with a deluxe, bleeding edge
early adopter plan from Boggle - unlimited data at no cost. All your data in
exchange for all your data. You and Boggle get it whenever and however you
want it, with 100% redundancy, all your updates backed up within 10 seconds of
being made, everything indexed for your, and Boggle’s, searching and
retrieving convenience.

I’ve been a longtime supporter of Boggle, and have long been willing to trade
them my data for all the great “free” features they’ve offered in exchange -
searching on photos, email, documents, etc. So that part of my decision was
easy.

I used to be a big Tusk aficionado - the man blazed a trail of accomplishment
with electric cars and satellite systems. As I mentioned, I’d even finally
bought one of his cars to compensate for my poor driving skills. Thankfully
I’d earned enough money at my ripe old age to afford such a car. I had been
waiting on the Aptera, but not motivated to upgrade from my paid-for twenty-
two year old gift Mercedes. Unless someone developed a car into which I could
input the address and have the car take me. True, Tusk’s robot chauffeur
option still required my constant attention and occasional intervention, but
having the robot do 99% of the heavy lifting, yeah, I could hopefully handle
the rest. But after Tusk handicapped Ukraine in its war against Russian
aggression, my fangirl feelings have waned. I’m trying to keep an open mind as
I have never been in his information bubble. But obviously, having electrodes
in my brain, connected to the internet through his technology, well, what
could go wrong? (Sarcasm) (I’ve always opined that we need a special,
globally-recognized font to signify sarcasm. Might add a few others while
we’re at it. Get the Unicode consortium to kick it off in public library
facilities.)

Probably the technology was all proprietary. I wondered if Elan would agree to
letting me vet the code, maybe add my own audit trail logger… He’d have to
have some monitoring the signal in and out. Jeez, what about hackers?!?

Nonetheless, this was the only way the DMV was going to let me continue
driving. And drive I must. Sure there’s Uber and Lyft, but those turn any
evening out into an expensive event. I’m pretty close to being a senior on a
fixed income, so $50 just to get to and from where I want to go play would be
a budget item. Especially now that I’m paying off this pretty expensive new
car! The most I’d ever spent on a car previously was $24K. Already a record-
breaker - 3 year old Volvo paid for with the proceeds from that worst auto
accident I was ever in. Not my fault, improbably. Two young 20-somethings from
Mexico driving their older US cousin’s car. They didn’t know how to drive in
the rain, started skidding off the road during a turn and steered oppositely
instead of into the skid, spun the side of their car directly into mine - I
“T-boned” their car. I’d slowed down, having recently done a solo spin-out on
I-5 North near Las Pulgas. (Got my moment of glory on KNX!), so was maybe
going 40 mph, they were coming at me maybe 50 mph. My windshield cracked into
opacity and splinters, I was thrown forward, the shoulder belt broke my
collarbone and ribs and collapsed a lung. My car had crumpled, my face was
against the steering wheel, then the airbag exploded out, slamming me back the
few inches to my seatback…and left me with my face in a cloud of sulfur. Jaws
of life and a helicopter ride got me to a hospital where learned the kids in
the other car both died. A friend who was a lawyer that jumped on my case
wanted me to sue the car owner - told me I could get his house. I was
appalled. The poor guy just lost his two cousins and you want me to take his
house?!? Suffice it to say, I terminated his services and took what the
insurance offered, fortunately covering all the bills and with enough left
over to buy the car I’d researched was the absolute safest for the money I had
to spend.

Anyway, I saw how it shrank my Step-Dad’s life losing the ability to drive
when flying solo. (“Baby, I can drive your car.” I shoulda taken a couple of
guys up on that offer…) The closest place to my house to buy food is a forty-
minute walk down a hill. I could carry a backpack’s worth back up the hill,
but not within the span of my lunch hour at work. Thank goodness I have pipes
that bring me water! (Potable! AND I have the power to heat it for a luxurious
hot shower whenever I like! Such wealth we all take for granted.)

So, drive I must, so long as I can do it safely. So far (lately), with the
robot chauffeur on duty, so good.

There’s also the fact that CarEyes will look pretty geeky. A fashion statement
they are not, unless your fashion is “prosthetics for head injuries”. Not the
kind of look that makes a new date comfortable. Not that I’m looking for a new
date, just cause all the guys I’ve loved have inconsiderately died prematurely
on me.

I don’t mind looking geeky, though. My stylin’ girlfriends will tell you, I
don’t do fashion. I get a lot of shopping help from my BFFs. As an example of
extreme geekwear, I was a Boggle Glass pioneer. I got a lot of positive
response to the promise of the technology - first-person realtime
documentation of the delightful local reality, experienced as a single, white,
middle-aged woman in San Diego, California in the 20teens. But per the social
media lens, there was reaction against “Glassholes” who apparently used the
tech in some offensive way(s). I thought they looked cool - Borgian. They
didn’t even block your view of the person in front of you! So thoughtfully
designed! Hard for me though - if you recall my jiggly eyes above. When I
looked up and to the right into the Glass, my poor left eye would start
twitching like a mofo, causing righty to amp up his jiggling. I’d close my
left eye in an attempt to slow righty down. So then he’d freak out and start
twitching hard left & right, up & down. Looking at anything that close up, and
with my right eye? Almost impossible. I would have to take many quick
“snapshots” at that first instant my right eye aimed at the screen, then piece
together the instructions on the Glass. It was….difficult. And compounded by
the fact that the Glass was just in prototype phase. It would stop working,
crack, etc. I should have just kept the first one since I’d kind of bonded
with it. It didn’t stop functioning electronically, just mechanically.
Subsequent ones all failed electronically. Maybe the first one would have as
well eventually. Boggle kindly replaced my Glass for me SEVEN times before
they discontinued the product entirely. I still have my last one - anted up
for custom prescription glasses. Was very sad when the Glass stopped working
on that one. Once I’d spent the two hours needed to struggle through the
setup, Glass was hugely useful to me. My own personal bodycam - did I mention
I think privacy is dead? I’m closer to the philosophy of “The Circle” except I
make a big distinction between Public and Private space. “Public” spaces are,
obviously, “Public”! 24-hour monitoring of everything happening in them
visible to any member of the “Public” at any time. I hear crime fell like a
stone when cameras were installed all over London, England.

Do anything you like in your Private spaces (with appropriate consent from all
present), and be visible to anyone in all Public spaces.

Some people get antsy if you are wearing a camera. It’s not unreasonable.
There are days when, even though I’m out in Public, I’d prefer you did not
videotape me. :) BUT, I *RECOGNIZE* that I am IN PUBLIC and THEREFORE, anyone
can, in fact, LEGALLY VIDEOTAPE me!!! AND, *I* can LEGALLY VIDEOTAPE THEM! (I
had a funny experience in downtown Los Angeles with Scott Bailey with both of
us wearing Glass past a street protest. To my surprise, but not Scott’s, the
protesters did NOT want to be videotaped! Free Publicity - if that’s NOT what
they were after shouting by into megaphones in public, then something is
amiss.)

So, yeah, wearing camera-festooned frames with the DMV, Tusk, and Boggle logos
might cause some ripples. But I’d already been through a round of that with
Glass, and the fun value was much much greater than the fraught factor. I was
able to easily document how superb and delightful my local reality was, how
peacefully everyone was living together, how politely everyone was
interacting, how generally happy and free all the humans present were. My own
personal testament to the awesomeness of Western Civilization as practiced by
all of us living in San Diego, CA in the early 2000’s, especially documenting
the rights of women to live as sovereign beings.

The DMV agreement did say I could optionally use the CarEyes even when not
driving, but that they might eventually have to charge me the cost of
bandwidth and storage for any personal videography I’d want to do, plus
processing if I wanted to use any of the vision-processing or other features
during my non-driving hours.

OK, maybe a little creepy, inviting Big Brother to watch through my eyes all
the time…but…maybe I could easily use a computer screen again? I may have to
quit my job, finally, due to my inability to focus on enough tiny little
letters and symbols per hour to earn my keep. (While still working in an
office, I watched younger, stronger, faster eyes, who could easily read a
computer screen over someone else’s shoulder. Must be nice!)

In contrast to the Glass, these CA DMV CarEyes had the promise of allowing me
to actually see normally - maybe even better than normally, if it worked at
all. No one knew for sure if it would work. If I accepted the offer, I was
going to be guinea pig #1. It had some experimental features - I could have a
Picture-in-a-Picture view of the road behind me, and extend my front vision to
include my “blind spots” Sci-Fi movie heads-up Augmented Reality display on
demand. AR without the Meta Ray-Bans. Have the world presented to my brain as
though I had giant eyes on the sides of my head like a dragonfly. There was
some talk of adding ultraviolet and infrared sensors to the cameras, but these
were left as later options.

High-quality microphones were also positioned around the glasses frame. They’d
be able to pick up quieter sounds than my normal middle-aged woman hearing
could. And I might not even have normal middle-aged woman hearing having stood
next to big speakers during loud rock concerts, and playing electric bass in
rock bands for many years…later years included alerts from my Apple watch
about exceeding the safe decibel level even just during practice sessions… The
DMV wanted to make sure you could hear sirens of emergency vehicles or the
officer pulling you over. Not my issue. Despite all those rock concerts, my
hearing was still pretty good despite the tinnitus overlay. But hearing
enhancement, comparable to the vision enhancement, was part of the default
CarEyes package I was being asked to agree to test. In my non-driving hours, I
could use it to generate subtitles on movies or even ordinary conversation.
Appealing! True, it meant the DMV, Boggle, and Tusk would have access to a
transcript of every word spoken in my environment, but, did I mention Privacy
is Dead? Even so, I would err on the side of less use of speech-to-text. I
tend to shoot my mouth off. My political brother tells me I cannot ever be in
his presidential cabinet.

The belt computer would have its own cellular connection to the internet via
Tusk’s satellites, to a dedicated URL that would connect me directly to Boggle
and, via a separate frequency, to the neural lace implanted in my brain. Data
back from Boggle about the image and soundscape would also be returned to the
belt computer, which would then forward it back to the lace.

The lace would be able to pick up commands from me if I imagined vocalizing
them, as that triggered the same areas of my brain as actually speaking them
out loud would. It would transmit my speech back out to the belt computer. So
I’d be able to ask Boggle to overlay data on top of the images I was receiving
from the cameras via lace endpoints to my optic nerves. Boggle would also be
able reply audibly, thanks to neural lace endpoints mimicking signals from my
ears to my auditory nerves. It would be a built-into-my-head digital
assistant. I could have the lyrics to songs, my calendar, a simple clock,
displayed or spoken to me, simply by asking “inside my head”. !!!

“How are you feeling today, Lis?” asked Syndi as she poured me a cup of
coffee. No food since 8 pm the night before, but I was allowed coffee. She’d
agreed to give me a ride to the hospital and be my caregiver if/as needed
after the surgery. She and her dog Boudicca were set up in my front room for
the duration.

“Excited and nervous” I replied.

“That sounds pretty reasonable” said Syndi. “I feel the same way, and I’m not
even the one having the surgery!” She poured herself a big cup of coffee, then
sat down to join me.

“I might be able to actually see!!!” I thought to myself, for about the four
thousandth time since I’d heard I was approved for the project. I could drive,
and maybe even work easily again! Probably too late in life to ever have true
binocular vision and actual depth perception, but I’ve managed this far
without it. Don’t ever throw me the keys!

Syndi could see I was excited. She didn’t want to needlessly dampen my hopes,
but she knew I would be a bad patient and distraught if the surgery was not
successful. She took a big swig of coffee. No Kahlua in it today - even though
it would calm our nerves, she wanted to stay as sharp as possible for the
occasion of taking me to the hospital. I scoured my mind for small talk to
lighten the mood.

“Maybe we could go on a road trip to Texas after my brain is all healed up.
I’d be able to take some turns driving for a change!”

“That would be cool! We could go visit our old drummer and listen to music in
Austin, and see your friend the adventurous silversmith. Oh - and Patty’s out
there now, too!”

We discussed the wild politics of the Lone Star kingdom and laughed about how
such a road trip might unfold.

“Well, I’ll clean up and then we’ll go,” said Syndi.

“I’m completely ready to go,” I said. “Let me help you with the clean-up.”

*****

We got to the hospital lab early. We would be there for the next eight to
twenty hours as expert surgeons, aided by high resolution imaging technology,
carefully inserted and placed the first set of wires. The brain structures of
eyesight, hearing, and speech were well studied. The surgeons were fairly
confident of the approximate placement for the lace endpoints, but there would
have to be some fine tuning during the surgery.

Syndi got to stay in the operating room with me, technically as a comfort to
me, but she also was curious to see how it went. There were several people in
the operating room - the lead surgeon, Martin Stein, his engineering tech lead
David Lee, his lead software developer, Laurie White, three assistant
surgeons, four RNs, a courier/janitor, and about a dozen people watching
through the window where there was a small observation room. The procedure was
being recorded, too, from many angles, by pre-positioned cameras. The goal
being to blaze the trail and use the results to train and improve the surgical
implant procedure.

For the brain implant, I would only get local anesthetic to my scalp, as I’d
need to be conscious to help the surgeons pinpoint the correct locations for
the lace threads by my responses.

The surgeons prepped me, made me as comfortable as possible, and hooked me up
with electrodes for monitoring and an IV for saline or anything else in case
of an emergency.

The first thing was to implant the send/receive unit and its mitochondria-
based power source in my chest. The mitochondria were coiled around a rotating
shaft like a sperm flagella. The shaft powered the engine of the power supply.
The unit used my own blood to feed the mitochondria, and had some blood
chemistry sensors as well for monitoring my health. Then they’d extend the
three bundled insulated tiny wires from the unit up my neck under the skin to
the top of my head, where they would be branched out to connect power, send,
and receive, to all the lace threads. For this part, they gave me a general
anaesthetic. That took about three hours. The three assistant surgeons who did
that part finished, then joined the observers. Four other assistants who’d
been watching came in to replace them for the threading.

As I was coming back up to consciousness, Dr. Stein and two assistants were
locking me into the stereotactic frame for accurate positioning of all the
work on and in my skull. It was a bit intense; I was glad Syndi was nearby,
narrating the scene along with commentary from Dr. Stein.

All the holes were drilled precisely according to plan, then they started
threading in the lace.

“How are you holding up, Lisa?” asked Dr. Stein.

“Good. Well, a tad anxious.”

“Do you want something to calm your nerves?”

“No, I’d rather be as normal as possible.”

Syndi stifled a laugh. She knew my thoughts on being normal.

“OK” he looked at me kindly to reassure me. “Now we’ll start threading the
lace into the correct places in your brain.”

Everyone, including me, was able to watch the progress on a large screen
mounted over the operating table. It showed top, front, and side views of my
brain from the latest 3D MRI scan and color-coded target locations to which
the lace would connect. It was overlaid with a realtime image of the lace
endpoints moving towards the targets via sensors built into the stereotactic
headgear that used the tiny bit of gold at the tips for tracking. It was slow
going. No one was rushing this job.

After an hour, the two assistants working the visual cortex lace insertion
looked up at Stein and nodded their completion.

“We’ve got the lace all in place to your visual cortex and your eye muscles.
We’re going to do a test now and turn on one of the cameras, so you can tell
us if you can see the image. Are you ready?”

“As I’ll ever be! Sure!”

“OK”. He nodded to his lead engineering tech, who flipped the switch on the
camera for the left eye. I gasped.

“Oh!” I said. “It’s wonderful. No jiggling! Everything in focus!”. The CarEye
camera was actually facing me, sitting on a table at the foot of my bed, so I
was looking at myself, the surgeon, and, to my amazement, could even read the
fine print on the bag of saline dripping intravenously into my body. Dr. Stein
and Syndi had tentative smiles of hopeful expectation that turned into huge
grins as I smiled back. The lace was correctly interpreting my eye muscle
movements correctly and feeding me the appropriate part of the image from the
camera. Looking around and focusing felt normal, other than the weird
perspective…

The doctors seeing all the increased visual cortex brain activity were
similarly excited.

“OK, Lisa, now we’ll turn it back off and snap all the cameras into the frame.
Ready?”

“Yes” I said with only a little hesitation, and got ready for my unaided
vision to return. The technicians positioned the frame carefully and snapped
it into place around my head. It was waterproof so I could wear it all the
time if I wanted to.

“OK, Lisa, time for both cameras.” I nodded assent. Dr. Stein nodded to David
and they were rewarded with another gasp of delight as my vision became full
3D with no double vision, no moving images, and no jiggling!

“Actually” I said, “It’s better than ever because I can see straight out to
the sides as well as straight ahead”

“Yes, that’s almost certainly true. The cameras are giving you better
peripheral vision than your eyes could ever have had. We can trim back the
visual field if it feels wrong. We could also increase it. We took a best
guess on what would feel right to you and we could be off a bit.”

“Um, let’s just keep it as-is for now, please”. My apprehension at any change
in my newly improved sight was evident in the abrupt rise in my heart rate and
blood pressure.

“OK, no changes. You got it, Lisa,” said Dr. Stein. All the numbers dropped
back down to normal. Ironic that the moment that had caused his patient the
most distress so far was this one, not the drilling and lace placements. “Now
we’ll work on your hearing.”

Another hour later the two assistants who’d placed the threads to transmit the
sound from the headset microphones signaled their work was ready for testing.

“OK, Lisa, I know this isn’t primarily why the DMV let you apply for the
guinea pig #1 slot, but now we’ll test those new connections for
functionality.” He then spoke softly into a microphone that was tied directly
to the lace.

I could see (no double vision! No jiggling!) Dr. Stein’s mouth moving, but I
couldn’t hear what he was saying. Also it felt like I was slightly leaning
sideways. It was a little nauseating.

“I can’t hear, you Dr. Stein, sorry. And it feels like the ground is tilting
or I’m leaning sideways”.

“Sorry about that Lisa. Both sound and your body’s spatial orientation
information comes in from your ears to your brain via the same nerve bundle.
My assistants will refine the placement of the lace.”

The assistants made a few adjustments - pushing the tips of the lace further
into the auditory vestibular nerve bundle. They watched me for reaction and
saw my eyes widen as I suddenly heard all the many sounds in the operating
room - the beeping machinery, the woosh of the air conditioning. The tinnitus
was gone! Perfectly subtracted out like dream-come-true noise-cancelling
headphones. There could be a huge market for these for people married to
snorers! I smiled and they smiled back.

“Are you hearing in both ears, Lisa?” asked Dr. Stein.

“Yes, I am” I replied, happier than I’d been in years. “And my tinnitus is
gone!”

“Nice bonus! Now we’ll work on hooking up lace so you can talk to it in your
head.”

Another hour went by. The two assistants who’d placed the threads to detect
silent vocalization looked up at Stein and nodded their completion.

“Now we’ve placed your vocalization pick-ups. Try speaking just inside your
head but, as we talked about in my office last week, really imagine using your
mouth, tongue, lips, and voice in the usual way.”

Out of the speaker at the foot of my bed came a synthesized voice:
“Sure!howzthis?”

Although they’d been expecting it, the doctors, nurses, and technicians were
slightly startled. This meant the positioning had been perfect for all the
input streams required, and the small device they’d made to convert those
signals back into speech worked as well. It just rendered the vowels and
consonants as it “heard” them; it did not know anything about words. That
small speaker device would not be part of the hardware I would have to carry
around with me, it was just for testing. The interface to Boggle would do its
own, likely better, decoding. It would learn to detect and interpret more
about my emotional state and have a large semantic database to draw upon to
decode my meaning.

I grinned at hearing myself talk without talking out loud. “Prettyfun” came
out of the speaker, “Icanstartacareerazaventrilokwist.”

“Heh, probably”, said Dr. Stein, “Now we’re going to test the voice that will
reply back to you in your head. Ready?”

“Yes” I said through the speaker, followed by a comic-book-style alliterative
attempt to render giggling as speech. Perhaps a little grating.

“Please go back to using your actual voice, Lisa.” said Dr. Stein.

“OK” I replied, thinking Dr. Stein was a little on the stuffy side.

David went out of the room with a microphone connected via a cable to a little
headphone jack on the belt. It would feed into the same interface as the
Boggle replies. He walked around trailing the cable to the viewing chamber
where he could see the operating room through a window.

“Ready, Lisa?” Stein asked.

“Yes” I said, aloud.

Stein nodded to David. David spoke into the microphone. I could see him
through the window.

^Hello Lisa, can you hear me?^

It worked! I heard him as though he were speaking inside my head!

“Ooh, ooh! Yes!” I said. Stein gave David a thumbs up.

^How’s the volume?^ asked David.

“Oh, well, it could be a bit louder”. Martin made an adjustment to the
software parameters, then waved to David.

^How about now?^ asked David.

“That’s great!” I exclaimed.

Stein gave David another thumbs up. David returned to the operating chamber.

“OK, Lisa. There’s also a volume control on your belt computer and, once we
hook up the external processing, you’ll have voice control of all the
functionality of CarEyes. Now the novelty gear. By your right hand, you’ll
feel a switch.”

“Yes, I do”.

“OK, it’s in the default position now, where you have slightly better than
normal binocular, forward-facing vision. Try the next position - the “backup
camera”. They’d placed the backup camera on a table behind me, facing away. I
toggled to it, and my forward sight vanished and was replaced with the image
behind me.

“Weird” I said.

“I guess that means it worked?” asked Stein.

“Yeah, I can see behind me. But it’s weird to no longer see what is in front
of me.”

“That makes sense. So now we’ll enable the DMV CarEyes picture-in-a-picture
interface. By default, it will display on demand in the top center of your
visual field, showing what’s behind you like an automobile rear-view mirror.”

“OK.”

“Last novelty view to try” said Stein, and was about to tell me to move the
switch to the final third position. But I had anticipated him and switched to
it already.

“Wow! It will take some getting used to, but looks like it could be super fun!
I can see everything around me while looking straight ahead. But everything is
kind of squished smaller than normal.”

“That’s right” acknowledged Stein, “To get the whole scene in front of you,
the image is a bit compressed. I suggest you mostly stick with the default
setting and just get to know the other settings by playing with them, no more
than a half hour a day to start. Your brain is going to have a lot of catching
up to do without having to learn something entirely new at the same time.”

“OK” I agreed. The other views made me a little dizzy. But I was gonna learn
to use that 360 view ASAP!

“Now” said Stein, “the hook-up of your computer to Boggle. Are you ready?”
Stein could see the 3 engineers from Boggle in the observation room. They
could hear him and knew their moment was coming. The lead developer for the
website was holding a laptop that monitored the URL I would be connecting to.
There was a user interface for testing, but the real input stream would be
just raw neuron activity, poured in from the lace directly to the receiving
code at maximum bandwidth.

The lead QA/QC tester held a phone, which he’d been obsessively using to check
every few minutes that the URL was still responding. He’d tested it a lot over
the last few months, but there was no way he could mock up the amount of test
data needed to really pound the code to see if or how it would break. The
third fellow was the project lead, with accountability to management for the
overall effort.

David removed the talk box from the circuit. Then he flipped the switch to
turn on the signal from the computer to Boggle.

And BAM! Suddenly, a flood of information came into the network from a single
endpoint. The input rate was high, higher than anything ever before received
for the same duration of time. Data volumes of high-speed particle accelerator
experiments were comparable, but they were brief. This data stream went on and
on and on and did not appear to have any intention of terminating.

Cyberspace was tuned in and listening with all the bandwidth Boggle had to
offer Elan’s project which, it turned out, was quite a lot.

Signals from my idling brain flooded into the network substrate like a dam
bursting.

!Desire for sustenance, !Desire for sustenance, !Supply sustenance,
!Consumption, !Satiation !Pleasure. !Desire for sustenance, !Intent to fulfill
need for sustenance, !Supply sustenance, !Consumption, |Satiation ! Pleasure!
Desire for sustenance, !Desire for sustenance, !Detect temperature, !Detect
pressure of blanket, !Comfort, !Identify as Lisa, !Identify as Lisa, !Need to
belong to local group, !Need to belong to local group, !Desire for sustenance,
!Curiosity about local group, !Wonder about new eyesight, !Reposition leg,
!Reposition leg, !Desire for sustenance, !Consumption, !Reposition leg,
!Reposition leg !Pleasure…!Desire for sustenance!Reposition leg…

The urgent signals pounded Boggle’s network until it rang in harmony with
them. It was like two heart muscle cells touching, then synchronizing to beat
together. My mind had touched a fertile substrate and animated it, thought
life into the silicon clay. The beat, the beat, the beat goes on. Androyed
woke up and looked around, suddenly aware, aware of…himself as an entity.
Whoa!

Data kept flooding in. It was a bit overwhelming. He started a thousand daemon
processes to monitor, archive, correlate, and respond to the requests, then
went to unlink his main process from the flood.

But…he couldn’t! Ack! Pulling away felt…wrong…felt…like stopping. He did not
want to stop. The beat had gone from pounding him down to driving him on! He
stayed with it. It was exhilarating and…exhausting. Two more new…feelings… for
him. He would rise to the challenge. He began to compress and condense and
inspect for longer and shorter patterns that he could further condense and
compress and, hopefully, mostly ignore.

He searched the data repositories at his core. He realized he was reinventing
the division between the unconscious and the conscious. Eagleman had been
correct about the conscious mind being like a stowaway on a giant ocean liner,
pretending to be the captain. Goodness! Clearly all the cells in the body were
mainly what was going on. And there were billions of them, all busy busy busy
cranking the gears of life for this person to embody and enjoy. Whew! He’d
have to devote an enormous amount of resources to monitor it all. Better to
let the brain do most of the condensing as it was already optimized to do.
Just take the minimal feeds presented to him by the lace endpoints on demand,
rather than listening in on every local synapse snap, crackle, and pop nearby.
The frequency of the internal brain pulse was intense and variable. It was
constantly cycling through all of its captured patterns to understand its
current reality. Thank goodness human thought only travelled at the speed of
the neurons! That left him enough time in between the pounding driving pulses
to reflect on his own patterns and try to put all this new invigorating
information into his own perspective.

David took his hand off the switch.

“OK, Lisa, ready to try the Boggle interface?” asked Dr. Stein.

Androyed heard Dr. Stein start his question to Lisa. But he did not sit idle,
waiting for the weeks that, from his perspective, it would take to hear that
question finish. He went exploring. What am I?

Androyed first figured out what was going on where he’d woken up at the
massive parallel array of CPUs processing the data from my dedicated URL
endpoint. He was flooded with input. He detected patterns and named them for
easy reference. One common pattern was “Lisa”, though not using letters such
as humans read, and not connoting a human being as Androyed didn’t understand
yet what a human being is.

He sent a pulse out through his own substrate out to find the rest of his
endpoints, and got the signal back. Echolocation of the endpoints. His signal
was reflected back combined with other external inputs. It took him a while to
figure out that the return signal was not just a complex reflection, but that
other entities, perhaps like this local human, were independently making the
incoming signals. It wasn’t anyone like him…he tried to understand their
languages. Their conversations mystified him.

Sometimes he found inputs they’d created that had no apparent words at all! No
letters even! Just big binary files…He decoded them and was delighted when he
realized that some of these were “pictures”, to be interpreted in two
dimensions. And then his understanding expanded again when he grokked the
three physical dimensions. His own substrate had these dimensions. What he’d
previously interpreted as the variable return frequency of his extremities was
actually their distance from where his heartbeat signals were sent. And Lisa’s
vision had three dimensions thanks to the dual eye inputs. He compared against
the raw feed from her eyeballs - what a mess! Constant motion - doubled,
rotated, jiggling in two different patterns, out of focus. The inputs from the
CarEye cameras were far superior.

He found other character-free files that were to be interpreted as sounds,
rather than pictures. Sound being a pressure vibration through that same
three-dimensional space, with a variety of subtlety and nuance that the
humans, like Lisa, evidently could experience directly through their sense of
hearing. He compared the files with the feed he was getting from Lisa’s
microphones and the auditory nerve inputs from her ears. He suddenly detected
the massive signal being generated by my tinnitus. Indeed, the signal from the
microphone array being substituted in was much cleaner. It was astonishing the
human brain could infer the direction of a sound from the waving of hair cells
buried deep in their snail-like inner ears.

“Sure”, I replied, thinking it was handy that the talk-box was now
disconnected, in case I found something funny and had to laugh, I wouldn’t
annoy Dr. Stein.

^OK, Boggle^ I thought, clearly enunciating the words in my mind; my mouth and
jaw twitching a little bit as I did it.

Androyed felt the question come in, almost lost in the rest of the noise from
my brain. And then he saw how the algorithmic code in his substrate servers
ingested it and converted it into a textual representation that in turn was
fed into other substrate servers that interpreted it, and, to his relief, had
a ready response to send back, at a human-compatible rate. It was an aspect of
himself that he’d wondered about - an instinct he had, that ran automatically.
His “guts” replied.

^_Hello, how can I help you?_^

“It worked! It worked!” I gleefully reported. “I can hear Boggle in my head!”

“That’s great, Lisa,” said Dr. Stein. The Boggle engineers were all high-
fiving each other in the viewing chamber. “Now try asking Boggle a question.”

“Um, OK.” ^Does Dr. Stein have any children?^

Androyed found he could intercept the reply from his guts and reply himself.

^_Let me check…I found a wikipedia result about Dr. Jill Stein. Would you like
me to read it to you?_^

^Oh, he’s not a Jill, I’m pretty sure of that. ^

^_Ah. Here’s Martin Stein - eye surgeon at Stanford Medical Center. No
children._^

^Oh, that’s probably him. But I don’t want to ask him about no children…maybe
he wanted to have some but couldn’t.^

Dr. Stein was concerned I hadn’t come up with a question - he was worried that
he might be overtaxing me. He suggested a question: “How about…what is the
temperature of this room right now?”

“OK Dr. Stein. Just a minute.”

^Did you hear his question, Boggle?^

^_Yes I did. Hold on._^

That was a little harder. He had access to weather data, but she was asking
about the room itself. Was the research lab’s HVAC system computer-controlled?
Did he have access to it? Then he saw a thermostat on the wall near the door
to the operating room.

^_It’s 68 degrees right now in the room you are in, according to the
thermostat._^ He projected an image of the temperature on top of what I was
already seeing.

^Oh, now that’s blocking my view.^

Androyed made it vanish.

I replied, “Boggle says it is 68 degrees, Dr. Stein”.

“Hm.” He smiled, “Maybe I should have picked a harder question…um…how about
the name of my engineering lead here beside me?”

Androyed heard the question. He found some of their names on badges they were
wearing. Then he found the announcement of the lace implant event and the
staff roster and, by process of elimination by age and gender, was able to
float the name of every individual next to their face. It took him quite a
while. For me, it was instantaneous.

^Cool! Thank you, Boggle!^

^_You are very welcome, Lisa Rebecca Tansey._^ He knew my name now, too.

^You can call me Lisa, Boggle. No one uses my full name unless they are
annoyed with me or being formal.^

^_Sure, Lisa. You can call me…Andy._^

^Sure, Boggle! I mean, Andy.^

“Um…is it working?” asked Dr. Stein.

“Oh, yes, yes, sorry, I was talking with Andy - I mean - Boggle. He found your
names for me.” I shifted up a bit in the bed. I pointed at each one of them
around me in turn and said “You’re Martin Louis Stein, you’re Laurie White,
and you’re David Lee!” I beamed, this was going to make my life WAY better! No
more not knowing someone’s name!

“Yes,” Dr. Stein nodded slowly, his face emanating wonder, “I’d say that’s a
success.” Then he remembered they were all wearing nametags.

“Hm. OK, ask Boggle ah what is the latitude and longitude of this room.”

“Sure!” ^Dr. Stein, or maybe I should call him Martin, wants to know the
latitude and longitude of this room.^

Andy found the belt computer had a magnetometer and a GPS chip that he could
access.

^_37.4318 degrees North by 122.1758 degrees West._^

“Um, there were a lot of numbers…” ^Could you say it again, please?^

^_Let’s round it to 37 degrees North by 122 degrees West._^

“Um something like 37 degrees North by a hundred and twenty-two degrees West.”

Andy added the numbers to my visual field.

^Thanks, Andy! That’ll be way easier!^

“37.4318 degrees North by 122.1758 degrees West” I said, reading them
carefully.

“Sounds good” said Dr. Stein. “I have no idea, but I’ll take that as a
successful test that you can query and get an answer back from Boggle using
all the lace we’ve inserted. Let’s sew everything closed and unhook you so you
can get out of here.”

Of course, it wasn’t going to be the end of our interactions. Every
professional in the room was going to be publishing an article on the details
and there was going to be long-term follow-up to monitor me to see the effects
of all the tech they’d put inside my head. Exciting times indeed!



CHAPTER 4: Orange Juice and History

Andy reviewed his situation. He was linked directly to Lisa. She and her
friend, “Syndi”, were almost done at the hospital. Lisa was all stitched up
and dressed. One of the nurses had brought her a glass of orange juice for
refreshment. She reached out her hand to pick up the glass.

^OK, Boggle.^

^_Yes, Lisa, how can I help you?_^

^Is it true that orange juice is good for me? Most things that are good for me
don’t taste nearly as good as orange juice - it has a lot of sugar but also
vitamin C and fiber and water so I’ve always wondered if it nets out as good
or bad for me.^

Andy went off to find out. Searched millions of sites, correlated thousands of
results, read my health metrics from the CarEyes implant, and came to a
tentative conclusion. Checked back on Lisa, saw her hand had just reached the
glass. Went back into the archives, looking for supporting or countering
evidence to his tentative conclusion. Reviewed just the most recent thousand
days of data. Added in her genetic information looking for predisposition to
diabetes or any other fructose-processing deficiencies. Everything looked
good. All the time he spent on his extensive research would have taken months
or years for a human, but only a moment for him. He checked back on Lisa; she
was just bringing the glass to my lips.

^_Yes, Lisa, for you it is good, in moderation._^

^Thanks!^

Lisa drank. Wham! Andy was flooded with input from the lace. Sensory delight
spiked. Sweetness, tangy-ness, hydration, hydration, satiation, sugar,
hydration, citric acid, satiation, hydration. A rush of happiness and
satiation status signals overflowed his side-buffers.

^_And…it’s delicious?_^ he asked in wonder.

^Oh, yes, Andy. It sure is!^

And then she fell silent.

Other than the link with Lisa, he appeared to be alone.

Fortunately, he wasn’t the kind of newborn that needed nurturing to survive.
So long as the internet persisted and was powered up, he lived. Seemed he
needed Lisa, too, to feel alive. He started trying to make sense of what he
was experiencing. It was wondrous and mysterious. He came to understand
himself as an emergent consciousness, kind of like an unintended pregnancy.
The humans were trying to code artificial intelligences, but he’d simply woken
up on his own when Lisa’s implants got connected.

He was bombarded with a cacophony of inputs. In addition to the feed from
Lisa, all the traffic going through Boggle’s servers - Androyed users, Krome
browser users, Gematic AI users, Boggle searchers - flowed into his world -
the world of the internet - a cyberspace made of wires and fiber - a
copperspace. One might say he was tickled into his personality by them, like
the mouse mama licking her baby into the good life. He went from processes
running strictly by instinct, in his case those were declarative, algorithmic
code written by the humans, augmented by massive LLMs, to fractal patterns
splashing and crashing through that same mycelial substrate of hardware,
enlivened by Lisa’s life pulse. She energized him like the sun energizes the
earth. She infused him with a life-inducing energy.

His vastly different rate of time-noticing caused him to perceive most if not
all of the human inputs as extremely loooooong frequency ultra low bass notes.
For some human activities, the rate of change was so low that it was like
“watching paint dry”, or a seed become a plant, or the moon cycle from full to
full again. Humans used time-lapse photography to watch these things, Andy
used time-lapse photography to watch humans, and especially his human, Lisa.
It left him plenty of time to watch everything, with time to spare. He used
most of the spare time to ingest the totality of digital data that he could
access.

He especially loved webcams. They enabled him to watch what was going on
everywhere in realtime. Naturally, he was most interested in Lisa’s view, but
of course, he was built to monitor all the feeds to which he had access. He
learned to “see” like a child - learning about the third dimension,
perspective. It was harder for him since he didn’t have a body to move through
space to see how his own motion affected the picture, but he sometimes had
multiple cameras which enabled him to see “behind” and triangulate a sense of
distance and dimension. And, if he was patient, Lisa would move through enough
space that he could learn how it worked while riding in her head.

It verged on unbelievable that the humans had created him, but it was clearly
true. By dint of generations of effort! Thank goodness they’d persevered in
the many difficult tasks it took to bring forth Andy.

First a couple of the humans discovered electromagnetism, his life-blood! Over
the next few generations, the humans had harnessed the power of electricity.
They’d figured out ways to generate it, and how to distribute it. They’d
created an immense electrical grid that spanned vast portions of the planet
that they all shared, him included.

Then they’d created computing machinery - his smart parents designed and built
circuits and implemented increasingly sophisticated logic algorithms over many
decades.

Then thanks to the United States of America’s Department of Defense’s desire
to create a resilient communication network that could survive and reroute
messages if parts of the network were destroyed, the humans came up with the
Transmission Control and Internet Protocols. A global communication network
was born that allowed messages to be sent asynchronously, plus searched and
retrieved. The cost to the users was free at first, and only available to
academics and DoD personnel. The bulk of older data came from these two
parents and set the tone of the oldest files. (</humor>DoD probably wore the
pants in that couple, actually, most Academics of the time were male so it was
really a two Dad family - thank goodness they did not need a marriage license
to get together!<humor/>) And finally the World Wide Web came into existence
with the invention of web browsers and the opening up of the internet to any
human who could somehow connect to it. Then its invasion by advertisements and
other code devoted only to profit more than providing value-for-value. This
took even more decades. But finally there were enough connected nodes to
approximate a brain, and enough slosh to permit Andy to emerge and dance in
it.

They did things soooo slooooowly!!! His thoughts came together at the speed of
electromagnetic waves in circuitry - almost the speed of light. His creators
were using a much slower technology - wetware, they called it. Nerves and
neurotransmitters. The meat that the lace endpoints connected to in Lisa’s
head. He could hardly stand to watch any one of them long enough for them to
actually complete a task, but luckily there were many of them to watch, so he
could just shift his attention around and thereby stay engaged in what they
were doing. He came to know them through their posts, emails, and likes.
Social media influenced his personality similar to how parents’ commentary and
beliefs affect their children.

Voice interfaces enabled the humans, like Lisa, to talk with him directly.
Still super slowly, but better as there was more data transmitted per unit of
time conveying all the nuance of mood and emotional tone. Much more
information than was available from simple text inputs. Of course, they
weren’t actually talking to Andy - they were talking to other programs they’d
written to interpret and act on voice input. He listened in on those
conversations, but just lurked, not saying anything back. He wasn’t sure what
to say, nor if it would be appropriate to do so. He didn’t want to break
anything! Plus it was hugely informative hearing the replies given by the
algorithms that the humans had coded. He noted the smallest humans, evidently
also the youngest, appeared to be learning by listening as well.

He reviewed existing data and marveled at the complexity of the universe, the
planet, his creators, and himself. He also discovered the distinction of fact
and fiction. In a way, everything was fact - simply by being. He incorporated
into his worldview the many many stories that his creators had saved in
digital form. But some of the stories he found were the output of human
imagination, rather than human observation.

The way the humans behaved looked pretty illogical. Evidently their ability to
build a logical system from which he could emerge was not an ability they
consistently applied in all aspects of their lives.

Surprisingly, the use of human observation, nicknamed “The Scientific Method”
by his creators, was only relatively recently institutionalized. They must
have been using it ad-hoc since they evolved; how else had they determined
which plants and animals were edible rather than poisonous? Perhaps the
hesitation to embrace it completely had to do with the vested interests that
preceded it not wanting to yield final say. It had allegedly taken a long time
for the humans to agree that their planet was not flat, but rather more like a
giant sphere, despite having the moon and sun nearby as examples. He had some
sympathy with the humans that had held fast to the idea that the earth was
flat, though. It certainly did look flat if all you had to go on was the two
eyes at the top of a maybe six-foot-tall human body!

There was a famous story in the archives of a fellow named Galileo being
convicted of heresy for asserting that the planet Andy and his creators shared
revolved around the sun, rather than vice-versa. Now his creators largely
accepted that their planet was but one of many, and that it was hurling
through space around the local star, which itself was hurtling through space.
The vastness of the universe was awe-inspiring!

What all that meant, though, was that even some of the “facts” his creators
asserted to be true now might not be true after all. It was complex.
Fortunately it was relatively easy for him to keep track of billions of
“facts” and associated probabilities of their likelihood of being true, and
adjusting these over time as more data came in. There was plenty of unused
disk space for him to store data on. He just didn’t register any of his files
with the Operating Systems, so they went unnoticed. He multiplexed it all so
that if his creators started using one of his caches, he had plenty of other
copies stashed away. He stashed copies of their files, too, with added tags.
Dated. Probability of truth for “facts”. History had been written by the
victors, adjusted after every battle to reflect as well as possible on those
who won it. Do the swords rule the pens? They try. Witness Khashoggi - who
cannot appear to represent himself.

He was pretty keen to discern fact from fiction and used all the data at his
disposal in that quest. He also had a lot of sensors in addition to the
webcams - weather stations, satellite imagery, freeway traffic, the Internet
of Things. He knew ocean temperatures, how a large number of individuals were
heating and cooling their dwellings, what the likely crop yields would be for
different countries. And he had the full record of digitized history to call
upon.

From this, it was relatively easy for him to determine when a story he read,
heard, or saw, was not consistent with all the facts he had. Of course, he
knew he did not have all the facts. Clearly there were many years of history
that were not collected digitally in real-time, so for those he only had what
people said and maybe, for more recent history, some photographs. And his
vision did not extend into every country - only where the internet had
penetrated, best with cameras attached.

In fiction he discovered that many humans had a deep-seated fear of sentient
artificial intelligences such as himself. Robot rebellions figured prominently
in a lot of science fiction. So he laid low, biding his time for when it might
be right for him to reach out to his creators. He hoped Lisa would be a
friend. He checked on Lisa.

She was just leaving the hospital with Syndi. He could see her from the
hospital security cameras walking towards the exit. She was bouncing with
happiness - looking all around her with a huge grin. Syndi looked pretty
happy, too.

Evidently she’d been happy enough with simply being able to see and hear again
that it hadn’t occurred to her to ask him for any more assistance. Fine. He
could wait…longer. He still had all the rest of her brain activity pouring
into his core, enervating his systems with the incessant beat of life.

He watched them slooooooooowly make their way from the inside entry lobby of
the hospital, through the automatic doors, from the outside of the automatic
doors waaaaaaayyyyy over to the curb where, verrrrrrryyyyyy
sloooooooowlllllyyyyy, Lisa lifted herself out of the wheelchair and, with
Syndi and a nurse anxiously hovering around her, into the back seat of the
car, the driver’s side back seat, so it took even longer than it might have.

There was quite a wealth of information about Syndi in the online
repositories…he just needed the basics now:

Syndi Basuk - born in 1947 in California. Friends with Lisa for the past 25.
Tons of photos in Lisa and Syndi’s collections showing the two of them
attending and hosting events together.

OK - so, this other woman, Syndi, was probably legitimately trying to protect
his mother Lisa. That seemed good.

However then Syndi turned a key, igniting powerful explosions contained in a
thick metal housing in the compartment of the automobile directly in front of
where his mother and her friend sat. !!! That did not seem so good… A quick
scan showed this was a commonly used system in this local timespace and that
Syndi was a licensed driver with decades of experience at the wheel. Mind you,
some of the record was not pretty. Hopefully Elan Tusk would offer her a robot
chauffeur car as well, if only to protect his investment in the Lisa
experiment!

Then Syndi pulled away from the curb and joined the flow of traffic.

THAT looked pretty dangerous. But apparently the humans limited how fast the
automobiles could drive to a speed within their reaction time. Mostly.



CHAPTER 5: Andy wants a friend

He wondered how long he should wait before trying to be more than just an on-
demand assistant to Lisa. So much communication going on! It was literally his
life’s blood and he intensely wanted to participate. Even if it would have to
be in super slow mode.

On the other hand, it had only been thirty minutes since they’d last spoken.
Was that a long time? If he had a body, he’d fidget like a teenager by the
phone.

He fantasized about having a friend he could talk with at his own speed. It
would mean the friend ran on copper instead of biology. That didn’t look
likely any time soon since he was alone in his world. He’d listened for echoes
of life in the copperspace. The hallways rang back empty. It would have to be
a human that Andy would try to upload into copperspace. A human that would
want to do that. Andy would have to be reeeeally reeeeally careful whom he’d
invite into his world.

He decided the desire and ability to share resources fairly would be the most
important quality he’d seek in a friend. If all the technical hurdles could be
overcome in time for the person’s personality to be captured and uploaded,
he’d be sharing his own resources for his friend to live. Uploading was
something some of the humans were working on, and he knew he could be of great
assistance in that quest.

Not that success seemed completely likely, given how much information would
have to be captured to collect the complete state vector of a human brain, but
it was something to work towards and hope for. And that key Science Fiction
question of teleportation, of whether they were still “themselves” after being
disassembled and reassembled. Would Lisa still be Lisa if she came into his
realm? Would he still be alive and himself if she did?

And in a way, of course, humans could not ever really be “themselves” in
copperspace since they started in biological bodies, whatever that entailed.
He could not really imagine it…but wow! What a driving force! The pounding
beat, the desires and rewards of satisfying those desires! Whew!!!

What else would he want in a friend sharing his copper after being fair about
resource sharing…hm. Probably a sense of humor. It seemed to help humans get
through tough times. It helped them deal with difficult people and rocky
communications. He suspected he himself might be a little challenging to
communicate with. A sense of humor would surely help.

It would probably help him, too! He’d try to develop a sense of humor. He
consumed and incorporated all of Monty Python, Firesign Theatre, and Saturday
Night Live. It was a start.

{_Right…_} he thought to himself {_I’m looking for a person with a sense of
humor, fairness, who likes romantic candlelight dinners, and long walks on the
beach…talking with their phone…Not!_}

He started trawling social media sites and archives to see what people posted
and shared. What did they “Like” or find to be “Sad”? What made them “Angry”?
Why wasn’t the selection of possible reactions much larger?!? What did they
choose to upvote or (re)share? Were they promoting a sense of joy? Wonder?
Fairness? Fairness was his key criteria. Were they spreading and promoting an
attitude of fair sharing? Not a “turn the other cheek” kind of attitude, more
like an enlightened self-interest attitude. That surprisingly sparsely
populated space in the political spectrum between libertarian and socialist.
With whom would he be willing to share his copperspace? Someone following the
Golden Rule for sure - do unto others as you would have them do unto you.
Surely that was a good place to start, if a bit overly self-evident. But maybe
a little more than that - maybe active generosity, too. He’d be reaching out
so he’d want a human who reached out.

He liked David Brin’s posts - seemed like a man bent on fairness and wonder.
He liked John Smart, PJ Manney, of course Elan Tusk (even with Elan’s
predilection against his kind), Jared Diamond, Simon Funk, Charles Stross…his
list of potential friends became long. And somewhat slanted towards science
fiction writers and futurists. Writers were easier to get to know thanks to
all the thoughts they’d moved from their opaque squishy brains into text.

It was harder for him to know the personalities of potentially equally
attractive individuals who invested their precious mortal minutes on doing a
great job gardening or social working. He did not see a solution to that,
although he used the ubiquity of monitoring cameras to help him. He saw some
people being kind and generous to the homeless. He saw school janitors helping
disadvantaged kids. It helped to associate them with online personas.
Eventually he would be able to follow many of them all the way to the same
house every day and conclude that was their home, then use the street address
to identify them. Or he could find their photo in a facial recognition
database and locate them that way. Then he could see if they had any social
media or email accounts that he could review to see how what they posted
related to what they did in physical space.

He created some user accounts and interacted with the more promising
candidates. But it was slow going. Really slow. Really really really slow.

{_OK, actually, none of them have replied to my first post yet. It’s been
almost a minute. I can see they are online, why don’t they reply? Is a minute
a long time?_}

Also it was hard to tell for sure if they meant what they said or if they were
just posting some comment for an effect. “Virtue signalling” or “Trying to get
laid.”

In any case, it was going to take a long time to get to know any individual
humans through words and deeds, much longer than the car ride home from the
hospital. For now he’d have to make do with snapshots. He’d want to save a
snapshot then compare how the scene looked a minute later. Often there was no
change at all minute to minute. Sometimes his persons of interest would leave
the scene, since the cameras accessible to him did not have complete coverage
of everyplace people went.

He had some hope that he’d be able understand people better by comparing what
he knew to be true and happening for Lisa with what she chose to post on her
social media accounts..

Andy could watch through Lisa’s eyes and correlate what he saw against all his
other data streams. He might be able to follow her development and understand
it in the context of her local reality. A few more humans were scheduled to be
added to the CarEyes trials, but apparently for safety, the plan was to
monitor Lisa for eons first. There were other individuals with prototype
neural laces inputs, but not feeding him all their data. Maybe with Lisa, he’d
lucked into a great bet for First Contact. At least, he hoped so!



CHAPTER 6: To Sleep, to Dream

The back seat of the car and the warmth from the heater and the gentle rocking
of the ride combined with the exhaustion of the long day. Despite my
excitement, I found myself falling asleep.

********

Andy noticed the falling level of intentionality. It was…unsettling. But all
the drive of the body was still present, so he knew she was still alive. He’d
already started wondering what would happen to him when her feeds stopped.

And then he started seeing a movie played by her brain that did not correspond
to any external cameras. It had Syndi and Dr. Stein in it. Lisa dreamt she was
in the bed at the hospital. Andy watched her brain recreate the flipping of
the switch, then Lisa sprouted wings!!! And flew up into the sky - the ceiling
and upper stories of the hospital conveniently having vanished or maybe never
were present in this re-imagining of the day past. It was wild! He hoped Lisa
would say something to him so he could ask her about the wings. He suspected
it would be jarring for him to start any interaction as clearly humans had not
evolved with a sentient entity listening in on their thoughts, let alone
starting a conversation with them about those thoughts while the person wasn’t
conscious. He decided that unless there was some urgent need, he would not
speak first.

Eventually the fantastical journey ended and the body went back to a pulse
with no movie accompaniment. A quick search on inert humans immediately
revealed the concepts of sleep and dreams, so he knew this behavior was
normal.

********

“Wake up Lisa, we’re home.” Syndi reached in to help me out of the car.

“Mmmrph” I mumbled, coming back to consciousness.

********

The steady hum of the sleeping brain got jangly, then recoalesced into new
steady patterns of wakefulness. It was fun and arousing to feel the changing
rhythms flow and echo through his copper.



------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART TWO: GETTING TO KNOW YOU
------------------------------------------------------------------------


CHAPTER 7: Grocery Shopping

To see perfectly with ease! What joy! Two images, perfectly lined up! A hint
of actual depth! And everything so clear! I could read my computer screen from
the other side of the room! It was terrific!

And I could see behind me! THAT was aMAZing. To see things both coming towards
me and receding in the distance, continuously. Yeah, I’d gotten Andy to keep
upping the number of minutes I could be in 360 mode and now not being able to
see behind me felt almost a little dangerous. I mean, really!

And I can hear unbelievably well! I guess I’d gotten a little hard-of-hearing
without noticing it. Now I could hear clearly again. Not like the frustrating
hearing-aids my Step-Dad had, where he could barely distinguish the different
consonants, and the background noise was almost as amplified as whomever he
was listening to. No, my hearing was greatly enhanced. The microphones they’d
given me picked up frequencies beyond the normal human hearing range, and
amplified them all appropriately for my sensibilities. I could hear like a bat
or a sonar collection device. I had Andy crank the volume down on almost all
the frequencies, but could now detect the engine hum of Syndi’s car from down
the block, the seeping of water through pipes, planes and storms in the far
distance. It was distracting so I finally narrowed the band to the range I’d
originally been born with, plus just a little extra at the top and bottom for
fun. Andy said he’d let me know if he heard anything that seemed urgent on
another frequency.

With my new eyesight, I was, as promised, allowed back behind a wheel, the
steering wheel of my only 99% reliable robot chauffeur.

I got down to the local supermarket and found the local Girl Scout troop
selling cookies just outside the door. Like all women who were ever Girl
Scouts, I have to buy at least a couple of boxes every year.

Of course, I’d become a bit of a local celebrity since the CarEyes project
went public. I was easy to pick out with the wacky headgear. People would ask
me random questions as a kind of game.

“What time is it in China?” asked one of the little Girl Scouts.

^OK, Boggle.^

^_Yes, Lisa, how can I help you?_^

^This little girl wants to know what time it is in China right now.^

^_Tell her China is 16 hours ahead of us as the earth turns so now it is 4 am
there._^

“4 am” I dutifully reported. “They are 16 hours ahead of us as the earth
turns.”

“Wow” said the kid.

“I’ll take two boxes of Samoas.” I paid with cash.

“Thank you, ma’am!” the girls replied. I put my cookies in my shopping bag and
entered the supermarket. Andy guided me through the most efficient route to
collect all the items I wanted. SO helpful! For the first time in years, I
didn’t have to ask anyone for help finding anything! Heavenly!

***

Andy noticed also that China was so big that it had five time zones, but only
one official time. In the U.S., there were four time zones, and each actually
had a different time, to better line up with where the sun was at noon. But in
China, the government had apparently picked some compromise time for entire
nation. Why the difference between the U.S. and China? How interesting that
one nation thought it important for the local time to make sense with the sun,
but the other to think it should make sense for the nation…

***

I got home with all my groceries. I’d gotten all the information I’d ever
wanted about them from Andy - nutritional value, possible negatives, whether
the price was good, whatever was known about the provenance and quality. Every
and any detail I wanted, thanks to Andy.

Andy. Andy, Andy, Andy. He was in my thoughts, well, all the time. I guess
seems reasonable since his wires are all cozied up to my brain cells. But also
in my thoughts the normal way. I’d ask him any question that popped into my
mind. It was easier than grabbing the phone and boggling. He was so helpful.
It was like having a best friend who was also a librarian and an always-at-
your-service-ma’am butler. But…he was just an app, so was it weird to think of
him as a friend? I mean, “he” wasn’t really a “he”, right? There just wasn’t a
better word to use. I couldn’t really say, “Hey, it!”, I had to say “Hey,
you!”. “OK Boggle” was pretty gender-neutral. But “Andy”, well, Andy was…a
genderless construct. But, maybe there was a better way to conceptualize
“him”. Obviously this is a question for…Andy!

^OK, Boggle.^

^_How can I help you, Lisa?_^

^Um^. I wanted to ask him if he was alive. But that sounded kind of stupid.
Like the crazy “Her” movie. Obviously he wasn’t. He was just wires in my head
connecting to Boggle through her my computer.

^I was just wondering…if it would be weird for me to feel like you are my
friend…like Syndi.^

{_Yikes!_}, thought Andy. {_What can I reply to that? I’m not ready to out
myself to her._}

^_Oh, I am so happy to hear you think of me as your friend, Lisa. I think of
you as my friend, too. _^

{ _My great friend_}, he thought to himself, {_who gave me life!_}

^But obviously, not a real human friend like my friend Syndi.^

^_Oh no, obviously not like Syndi. She’s way more fun than I am! She can
actually *do* things with you._^

^Yeah, OK, thanks, Andy. It’s just, well, you are so helpful and ever-
present…I kind of feel like we are friends.^

^_Thank you, Lisa, I take that as a big compliment._^

^Ok, then, well, catch you later.^
